Electromagnetic Proximity Effect: Superconducting Magnonics and Beyond
Tao Yu, Xi-Han Zhou, Gerrit E. W. Bauer, I. V. Bobkova

Abstract
The exchange interaction at interfaces between superconductors (SCs) and ferromagnets (FMs) has been a central topic in condensed matter physics for many decades, starting with the prediction of exotic phases such as the Fulde–Ferrell–Larkin–Ovchinnikov states and leading to the discovery of triplet superconductivity. This review focuses on new phenomena in SC—FM heterostructures caused by the non-contact dipolar interaction between magnons, i.e., the quanta of spin wave excitations in the ferromagnet, and the superconducting order. A universal non-relativistic spin-orbit coupling locks the polarization and momentum of their evanescent stray magnetic fields and leads to chiral screening by proximate superconductors. The interaction-induced hybrid quasiparticles are magnon-Meissner collective modes, magnon-cooparon, Josephson plasmonic modes, and nodal magnon-photon polaritons. Superconducting and normal metallic gates modulate and control the magnetodipolar interaction and thereby magnetization and energy transport at interfaces and in thin films. Here we review our understanding of dipole-coupled SC—FM systems obtained in the past five years, including the chiral gating of magnons in magnetic films by superconducting or metallic gates, proximity effects such as enhanced magnon transport, novel magnonic crystals, superconducting magnon transistors, ultrastrong magnon-photon coupling, and composite quasiparticles of magnons screened by triplet Cooper pairs. We emphasize the need for a self-consistent treatment of the coupled magnon-Cooper pair dynamics in solving the coupled Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation and Maxwell equations. By replacing London’s penetration depth with the microwave skin depth, we capture the difference between the screening by superconducting and highly conducting metals. The normal metal—ferromagnet bilayer system dynamics can then be represented by a non-Hermitian chiral Hamiltonian that leads to the prediction of a switchable magnon Peltier effect, a chiral magnon non-Hermitian skin effect, nodal magnon-photon polaritons, anomalous scattering of spin waves at semi-transparent potential barriers, and dipolar spin pumping. Finally, we point out the new functionalities offered by SC—FM devices for both classical and quantum information technologies and present perspectives for future research.

1
Introduction

The spontaneous emergence of order in an interacting many-body system at a critical temperature is arguably one of the most fascinating phenomena in physics
[
1
]
. The symmetry breaking associated with these phase transitions induces dramatic changes in the physical properties of the systems. Textbooks discuss, for example, the crystal formation out of liquid or gaseous phases, the superfluidity of atomic condensates, the superconductivity of metals, and the macroscopic magnetic/electric fields generated by ferromagnetism/ferroelectricity in solids, respectively.

Different orders may coexist in a friendly manner within a given material system, such as ferroelectricity and magnetism in multiferroic materials. On the other hand, magnetism is, in general, a foe to superconductivity since it suppresses Cooper pair formation, as illustrated in Fig.
1
. Superconducting ferromagnets only exist in exotic states such as the spatially modulated Fulde–Ferrell–Larkin–Ovchinnikov (FFLO) phase
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
, in the form of triplet Cooper pairs with finite orbital angular momentum intrinsically in materials such as uranium-based heavy-fermion superconductors
[
5
]
, in some other unconventional superconductors, including borocarbides
[
6
]
, ternary borides, chalcogenides
[
7
]
, and iron-based superconductors
[
8
,
9
]
, or in the form of a long-range proximity effect
[
10
,
11
]
.

Figure 1:
Overview of the main coupling mechanisms between a superconductor and a ferromagnet, viz. the
s
-
d
exchange interaction and the dipolar interaction. The exchange interaction either in the magnet or at the interface can lead to triplet Cooper pairing and the Fulde-Ferrell-Larkin-Ovchinnikov (FFLO) state. The dipolar proximity interaction gives rise to the Meissner and electromagnetic proximity effects.

Advanced thin film material growth and microfabrication techniques allow the study of heterostructures such as bi- and multilayers of differently ordered materials. In the case of ferromagnets (FMs) on superconductors (SCs), the contact exchange interaction dominates only at atomic-scale distances from the interfaces. The effects can still be drastic when the respective films have a thickness smaller than the superconducting coherence length. Ferromagnet(FM)—superconductor(SC) bi- and trilayers allow the controlled study of the LOFF oscillations
[
11
]
. Numerous reviews address the theory and experiments of equilibrium and transport properties of coupled FM
|
SC heterostructures
[
10
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. Novel multiferroic composites can be synthesized in the form of multilayers between ferroelectric and magnetic materials
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
.

Despite the enormous bulk of published research results, we perceive the need to review the developments in the study of SC—FM heterostructures that address new insights into the effects of non-local electromagnetic interactions. Diamagnetism gives rise to phenomena such as the Meissner effect and the levitation of superconducting disks on top of ferromagnets, as shown in Fig.
1
. On a microscopic scale, electromagnetic interactions are not considered important since conduction electrons efficiently screen the stray fields in equilibrium and low-frequency phenomena. However, the elementary excitations of the magnetic order, i.e., spin waves with quanta referred to as magnons
[
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
]
, are typically oscillating in the gigahertz (GHz) regime. Their stray fields can penetrate metals and superconductors over length scales that can easily exceed the thickness of ultrathin films. Since the response of the conductors in turn affects the magnetization, the dynamics of the entire system pose a self-consistency problem, also sketched in Fig.
1
. Over the past few years, solutions for various configurations and devices have revealed rich ramifications that are measurable and potentially relevant to applications, implying that the time is ripe to compile the evidence in a comprehensive review.

The present review focuses on the basic as well as novel physics of dynamical properties of multilayers that incorporate magnets, normal metals, and superconductors, with some discussion of ferroelectrics as well. It is ordered as follows. In Sec.

2
Magnetization dynamics and Cooper pairs

2.1
Magnetic order

The magnetic moment
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is a basic physical quantity in magnetism. Classically, it is the leading term of a multipole expansion of the stray magnetic fields of an ensemble of magnetic charges and electric currents.
The simplest magnetic moment
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{l}
is generated by an electron that moves in a simple loop. It is proportional to the orbital angular momentum
{\bf l}
via
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{l}=-|g_{L}\mu_{B}|{\bf l}
, in which
g_{L}
is the
g
-factor of the orbital magnetic moment and
\mu_{B}
is the Bohr magneton.
According to relativistic quantum mechanics, the intrinsic angular momentum of the electrons or spin
\bf s
, causes a spin magnetic moment
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{s}=-|g_{S}\mu_{B}|{\bf s}
, where
g_{S}
is the
g
-factor of the spin magnetic moment. In the non-relativistic limit,
g_{S}\sim 2
and
g_{L}=1
, and the total magnetic moment is the sum of the orbital and spin contributions
[
41
]

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mu}}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{l}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{s}\approx-{\mu_{B}}({\bf l}+2{\bf s}).

(1)

In magnetic materials, the electron spins order spontaneously to generate persistent magnetization distributions.
Inside a ferromagnet, the magnetization distribution
{\bf M}({\bf r})=\sum_{i}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{i}/\delta V
is an average over the
i
-th magnetic moment inside a mesoscopic volume
\delta V
. When a crystal field hinders the
formation of current loops and suppresses the formation of an orbital magnetic moment
[
42
]
, the magnetization
{\bf M}({\bf r})=-\gamma\hbar{\bf s}({\bf r})
is proportional to the spin density
{\bf s}({\bf r})
, where
-\gamma=-|g_{S}\mu_{B}|/\hbar
is the gyromagnetic ratio of an electron.

Magnetic moments interact with external and internal effective magnetic fields by a Zeeman interaction
H_{Z}
. When
{\bf B}_{0}=\mu_{0}{\bf H}_{0}
is the applied magnetic flux density in the magnetic medium, and disregarding internal fields for the moment

\displaystyle H_{Z}=-\mu_{0}\int{\bf M}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf H}_{0}d{\bf r}=\mu_{0}\gamma\hbar\int{\bf s}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf H}_{0}d{\bf r},

(2)

where
\mu_{0}
is the permeability of the vacuum and
{\bf H}_{0}
is the magnetic field strength, which is minimized by

{\bf M}\parallel{\bf H}_{0}
.

The main cause for magnetism is the exchange interaction, a modulation of the Coulomb interaction by the Pauli exclusion principle. In ions with open 3
d
or 4
f
shells, the exchange interaction favors finite local moments of spins that remain stable inside the condensed matter. But neighboring spins in a crystal also interact by an exchange coupling. According to the Heisenberg model, the free energy of an ensemble of spins reads

\displaystyle H_{\rm ex}=-\sum_{i,j}J_{ij}{\bf s}_{i}\cdot{\bf s}_{j},

(3)

where
{\bf s}_{j}
represents the electron spin at site
{j}
and
J_{ij}=J_{ji}
is the direct exchange coupling constant between spins at sites
i
and
j
. Since the atomic wave functions of local atomic moments are exponentially localized, the interaction is often significant only between nearest neighbors. In a simple crystal,
J_{ij}=J
is the same with all neighbors such that

\displaystyle H_{\rm ex}=-J\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{n.n.}{\bf s}_{i}\cdot{\bf s}_{j},

(4)

where
j
is summed only over the nearest neighbors of
i
. In reciprocal space

\displaystyle{\bf s}_{i}=\sum_{\bf k}e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{\bf r}_{i}}{\bf s}_{\bf k},

(5)

and
H_{\rm ex}=-J\sum_{i}\sum_{j}^{n.n.}\sum_{\bf k,{\bf k}^{\prime}}e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{\bf r}_{i}}e^{i{\bf k}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf r}_{j}}{\bf s}_{\bf k}\cdot{\bf s}_{\bf k^{\prime}}
.
For the simple cubic lattice with six nearest neighbors

\displaystyle\sum_{j}^{n.n.}e^{i{\bf k}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf r}_{j}}=e^{i{\bf k}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf r}_{i}}(\cos{k_x' a}+\cos{k_y'a}+\cos{k_z' a}),

(6)

where
a
is the distance between nearest neighbors, and

\displaystyle H_{\rm ex}=-2NJ\sum_{\bf k}(\cos{k_x a}+\cos{k_ya}+\cos{k_z a}){\bf s}_{-\bf k}\cdot{\bf s}_{{\bf k}},

(7)

where
N
is the number of spins.
When the magnetization modulation changes slowly on the scale of
a
, only small wave numbers
|{\bf k}|\ll 1/a
contribute to the energy. We may then expand the cosine term
\cos{k_x a}\approx 1-(k_{x}a)^{2}/2
and

\displaystyle H_{\rm ex}=-NJ\sum_{\bf k}(6-k^{2}a^{2}){\bf s}_{-{\bf k}}\cdot{\bf s}_{\bf k}.

(8)

More generally, for simple cubic, face-centered cubic, and body-centered cubic lattices with
Z
nearest neighbors

\displaystyle H_{\rm ex}=-NJ\sum_{\bf k}\left(Z-\dfrac{Zk^{2}a^{2}}{6}\right){\bf s}_{-{\bf k}}\cdot{\bf s}_{\bf k}.

(9)

In the long wavelength limit
{\bf s}({\bf r})
is effectively a continuous function of
{\bf r}
such that

\displaystyle{\bf s}_{\bf k}=\dfrac{1}{Na^{3}}\int d{\bf r}e^{-i{\bf k}\cdot{\bf r}}{\bf s}({\bf r}),

(10)

where
a^{3}
is the volume per spin. Moreover, we may replace
\sum_{\bf k}\rightarrow V/(2\pi)^{3}\int d{\bf k}=Na^{3}/(2\pi)^{3}\int d{\bf k}
.
Hence

\displaystyle-NJZ\sum_{\bf k}{\bf s}_{-{\bf k}}\cdot{\bf s}_{\bf k}=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{JZ}{a^{3}(2\pi)^{3}}\int d{\bf k}d{\bf r}d{\bf r}^{\prime}e^{i{\bf k}\cdot({\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime})}{\bf s}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf s}({\bf r}^{\prime})=-NJZs^{2},

(11)

where
s^{2}={\bf s}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf s}({\bf r})
is the constant squared modulus of the atomic spin, and

\displaystyle\sum_{\bf k}\dfrac{NJZa^{2}k^{2}}{6}{\bf s}_{-{\bf k}}\cdot{\bf s}_{\bf k}=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{JZ}{6a(2\pi)^{3}}\int d{\bf k}d{\bf r}d{\bf r}^{\prime}{\bf s}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf s}({\bf r}^{\prime})(-k^{2})e^{i{\bf k}\cdot({\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{JZ}{6a}\int d{\bf r}{\bf s}({\bf r})\cdot\nabla^{2}{\bf s}({\bf r}).

(12)

The total exchange energy in the continuum limit reads

\displaystyle H_{\rm ex}=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\left(\dfrac{-JZ}{6a}\right)\int d{\bf r}{\bf s}({\bf r})\cdot\nabla^{2}{\bf s}({\bf r}),

(13)

where
E_{0}=-JZNs^{2}
.
The spin
{\bf s}({\bf r})
is related to the magnetization
{\bf M}({\bf r})
via
{\bf s}({\bf r})=s{\bf M}({\bf r})/M_{s}
, where
M_{s}=\gamma\hbar s
is the saturated magnetization. Equation (
13
) can be interpreted as the interaction of the magnetization with an effective magnetic field
{\bf H}_{\rm ex}({\bf r})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle H_{\rm ex}=E_{0}-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{2}\int d{\bf r}{\bf M}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf H}_{\rm ex}({\bf r}),

(14)

where
{\bf H}_{\rm ex}({\bf r})=\alpha_{\rm ex}\nabla^{2}{\bf M}({\bf r})
and
\alpha_{\rm ex}=JZs^{2}/(3\mu_{0}aM_{s}^{2})
is the exchange stiffness parameter.
The exchange coupling constant is proportional to the Curie temperature
T_{c}

[
43
]

\displaystyle J=\dfrac{3k_{B}T_{c}}{2Zs(s+1)},

(15)

where
k_{B}
is the Boltzmann constant, so the exchange stiffness

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rm ex}=\dfrac{k_{B}T_{c}s^{2}}{2s(s+1)\mu_{0}M_{s}^{2}a}.

(16)

For example, the ferrimagnet yttrium iron garnet (YIG) has a cubic crystal structure with the lattice constant
A=1.2\times 10^{-9}
m. 40 Fe
3+
ions in a unit cell occupy 16 octahedral and 24 tetrahedral sites
[
213
]
with average volume
a^{3}\approx A^{3}/40
.
Fe
3+
has a half-filled 3
d
shell and spin
s=5/2
. According to Eq. (
16
) with
T_{c}=559~
K and
\mu_{0}M_{s}=0.177
T,
\alpha_{\rm ex}\approx 3.15\times 10^{-16}
m
-2
, which is close to
\alpha_{\rm ex}=3\times 10^{-16}
m
-2
reported in the literature
[
44
]
.

Not only do the neighboring spins interact with each other through the exchange coupling, but the long-range dipolar interaction couples the magnetization at a distance.
Indeed, the magnetization emits a dipolar magnetic field that spreads over a long range, which is governed by Maxwell’s equations
[
45
]

\displaystyle\nabla\times{\bf E}({\bf r},t)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{\partial{\bf B}({\bf r},t)}{\partial t},

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mu_{0}[{\bf H}({\bf r},t)+{\bf M}({\bf r},t)]=0,

(17a)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot{\bf E}({\bf r},t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\rho_{f}}{\varepsilon_{r}},

\displaystyle\nabla\times{\bf H}({\bf r},t)={\bf J}({\bf r},t)+\varepsilon_{r}\dfrac{\partial{\bf E}({\bf r},t)}{\partial t},

(17b)

where
{\bf J}({\bf r},t)
is the electric current of electrons,
\varepsilon_{r}
is the dielectric constant that depends on the materials, and
\rho_{f}
is the electron charge density.
For the ferromagnetic insulators,
{\bf J}({\bf r},t)=0
and
\rho_{f}=0
. Taking the curl of the second equation of Eq. (
17b
) and substituting it into Eqs. (
17a
), we find the stray magnetic field
{\bf H}({\bf r},t)
governed by

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}{\bf H}=-\nabla(\nabla\cdot{\bf M})+\mu_{0}\varepsilon_{r}\partial_{t}^{2}({\bf H}+{\bf M}),

(18)

in which the second term on the right-hand side is responsible for the radiation of electromagnetic field
[
45
]
.
For the magnetization fluctuation of long wavelength in the ferromagnets, the magnetization and its emitted stray magnetic field vary in the frequency of around tens of gigahertz and wavelength
\lambda_{s}\sim 1~\mu{\rm m}
, leading to the wave vector
k_{s}\sim 6\times 10^{6}
m
-1
. According to Eq. (
18
), the spatial fluctuation
\nabla^{2}{\bf H}\sim k_{s}^{2}{\bf H}\sim 4\times 10^{13}{\bf H}
is much larger than the time derivative
\mu_{0}\varepsilon_{r}\partial_{t}^{2}({\bf H}+{\bf M})\sim\mu_{0}\varepsilon_{r}\omega^{2}({\bf H}+{\bf M})\sim 10^{5}({\bf H}+{\bf M})
for
\omega\sim 100
GHz, assuming
{\bf H}
and
{\bf M}
in the same order. Therefore, it is safe to disregard the radiative time-derivative term in Eq. (
18
) such that the quasistatic approximation applies.
We notice that when the wavelength of the spin wave is comparable to that of light,
i.e.,

k_{s}^{2}\approx(\omega/c)^{2}\sim 2.6\times 10^{4}
m
-2
,
\nabla^{2}{\bf H}\sim\mu_{0}\varepsilon_{r}\partial_{t}^{2}({\bf H}+{\bf M})
, in which case the radiation of the electromagnetic field plays a vital role in the magnetization dynamics
[
46
]
. Accordingly, in the short-wavelength regime, the exchange interaction governs the dynamics of the magnetization, while in the long-wavelength regime, the dipolar interaction dominates.

By the quasistatic approximation, the magnetization fluctuation generates the stray magnetic field via

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}{\bf H}=-\nabla(\nabla\cdot{\bf M}),

(19)

which is generated by the magnetic charge
\rho_{M}=\nabla\cdot{\bf M}
.
It is then convenient to express the stray magnetic field in terms of the magnetic potential
{\bf H}=-\nabla\phi
, which leads to the Poisson equation

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\phi=\nabla\cdot{\bf M}.

(20)

With the magnetic scalar potential

\displaystyle\phi=-\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\int d{\bf r}^{\prime}\dfrac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot{\bf M}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|},

(21)

the dipolar field

\displaystyle{\bf H}_{\rm dip}({\bf r})=-\nabla\phi=\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\int d{\bf r}^{\prime}\dfrac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot{\bf M}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r-r}^{\prime}|}.

(22)

The dipolar interaction contributes to the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\rm dip}=\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{2}\int{\bf M}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf H}_{\rm dip}({\bf r})d{\bf r}.

(23)

We note that in the presence of SOC, there are other vital interactions in magnetism,
i.e.,

H_{\rm DM}
caused by the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI)
[
39
,
40
]
and
H_{\rm ani}
caused by the magnetocrystalline anisotropy
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
.

2.2
Magnons

Around the equilibrium magnetic configuration, the collective excitations in the ordered magnetic materials are spin waves, or their quanta, magnons
[
23
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
30
,
31
,
29
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
]
. Their typical frequencies range from gigahertz to terahertz scales.
Supposing a uniform magnetic field
H_{0}\hat{\bf z}
applied along the
\hat{\bf z}
-direction that biases the magnetization along the same direction, the transverse fluctuation
M_{x,y}({\bf r})\ll M_{z}({\bf r})\approx M_{s}
, where
M_{s}
is the saturation magnetization of the ferromagnets.
The magnetization
{\bf M}({\bf r})
is associated with the spin operator
\hat{\bf s}({\bf r})
via
{\bf M}({\bf r})=-|\gamma|\hbar\hat{\bf s}({\bf r})
. The spin is opposite to the magnetization with the
z
-component along the
-\hat{\bf z}
-direction.

To quantize the spin waves in the continuum model, we first use the Holstein-Primakoff transformation
[
47
]
with the bosonic operator
\hat{b}({\bf r})
to express the spin operators, which in the linear response regime read

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{+}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})+i\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})=\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r})\sqrt{2S-\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r})\hat{b}({\bf r})}\approx\sqrt{2S}\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r}),

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{-}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})-i\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})=\sqrt{2S-\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r})\hat{b}({\bf r})}\hat{b}({\bf r})\approx\sqrt{2S}\hat{b}({\bf r}),

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{z}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=-S+\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r})\hat{b}({\bf r})\approx-S,

(24)

in which
S=M_{s}/(\gamma\hbar)
.

\hat{b}({\bf r})
is expanded by the magnon modes labeled by “
p
” via mixing the magnon annihilation
\hat{m}_{p}
and creation
\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}
operators, i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{b}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})\hat{m}_{p}+{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}\left({\cal A}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\hat{m}_{p}\right),

(25)

where
{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})
and
{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})
are the amplitudes of the annihilation
\hat{m}_{p}
and creation
\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}
operators.
According to Eq. (
24
), the spin operators become

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sqrt{2S}}{2}\sum_{p}\left(\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}+{\rm H.c.}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sqrt{2S}}{2}\sum_{p}\left(i\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}+{\rm H.c.}\right).

(26)

Such a mixing of annihilation and creation magnon operators render the orthonormal condition for
{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})
and
{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})
different from that of photons and phonons.
The bosonic characteristic of
\hat{b}({\bf r})
leads to two relations

\displaystyle[\hat{b}({\bf r}),\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r^{\prime}})]=\delta({\bf r-r^{\prime}})\rightarrow\sum_{p}\left[{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p}^{*}({\bf r^{\prime}})-{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r^{\prime}})\right]=\delta({\bf r-r^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle[\hat{b}({\bf r}),\hat{b}({\bf r^{\prime}})]=0\rightarrow\sum_{p}\left[{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p}({\bf r^{\prime}})-{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p}({\bf r^{\prime}})\right]=0.

(27)

We consider the typical spin Hamiltonian composed of the Zeeman coupling to the external magnetic field
H_{0}\hat{\bf z}
, the exchange interaction, and the dipolar interaction:

\displaystyle\hat{H}=

\displaystyle-\mu_{0}\int d{\bf r}\hat{M}_{z}({\bf r})H_{0}-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{2}\int d{\bf r}\hat{\bf M}({\bf r})\cdot\left({\bf H}_{\rm ex}(\hat{\bf M},{\bf r})+{\bf H}_{\rm dip}(\hat{\bf M},{\bf r})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\gamma\hbar\int d{\bf r}\hat{s}_{z}({\bf r})H_{0}-\dfrac{\mu_{0}(\gamma\hbar)^{2}}{2}\int d{\bf r}\hat{\bf s}({\bf r})\cdot\left({\bf H}_{\rm ex}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})+{\bf H}_{\rm dip}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})\right),

(28)

where the exchange magnetic field
{\bf H}_{\rm ex}(\hat{\bf M},{\bf r})=\alpha_{\rm ex}\nabla^{2}\hat{\bf M}({\bf r})
, and the dipolar magnetic field [Eq. (
22
)]

\displaystyle{\bf H}_{\rm dip}(\hat{\bf M},{\bf r})=\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\int d{\bf r}^{\prime}\dfrac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot\hat{\bf M}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r-r}^{\prime}|}.

(29)

In terms of the equations of motion of the magnetization, we diagonalize the Hamiltonian and demonstrate the normalization condition of the mode “wavefunction”
{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})
and
{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})
.

We focus on the magnetic samples in which the saturation magnetization is uniform, such as the magnetic film, sphere, ellipsoid, and cylinder
[
214
,
215
,
216
]
, which with Hamiltonian (
28
) implies that the static dipolar field inside the sample is aligned with that of the saturation magnetization, since, otherwise, the saturation magnetization precesses around the static dipolar field. In this situation, the equilibrium spin distribution

\displaystyle s_{e}({\bf r})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}S,&{\rm{\bf r}\in\ the\ sample},\\
0,&{\rm{\bf r}\notin\ the\ sample},\end{array}\right..

According to the Heisenberg equation, the equation of motion of
\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})
and
\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})
from Hamiltonian (
28
) reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})=

\displaystyle-\mu_{0}\gamma H_{0}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})+{\mu_{0}(\gamma^{2}\hbar)}\left[\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm ex},z}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})\right)-\hat{s}_{z}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm ex},y}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},y}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})\right)\right],

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})=

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\gamma H_{0}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})+{\mu_{0}(\gamma^{2}\hbar)}\left[-\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm ex},z}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})\right)+\hat{s}_{z}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm ex},x}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},x}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})\right)\right].

(32)

In the linear-response regime,
{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})\approx{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(-S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})=-{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})
and
{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(\hat{\bf s},{\bf r})\approx-{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})
are static in Eq. (
32
), such that the fluctuated magnetic field is governed by

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07182pt{H}_{{\rm ex},y}(\hat{s}_{y}\hat{\bf y},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},y}(\hat{\bf s}_{\perp},{\bf r})=

\displaystyle H_{{\rm dip},y}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+\dfrac{\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})+\mu_{0}\gamma H_{0}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})}{\mu_{0}\gamma^{2}\hbar S}+\dfrac{\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right)}{S},

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07182pt{H}_{{\rm ex},x}(\hat{s}_{x}\hat{\bf x},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},x}(\hat{\bf s}_{\perp},{\bf r})=

\displaystyle{H}_{{\rm dip},x}({S}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+\dfrac{-\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})+\mu_{0}\gamma H_{0}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})}{\mu_{0}\gamma^{2}\hbar S}+\dfrac{\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right)}{S},

(33)

where the transverse spin fluctuation
\hat{\bf s}_{\perp}=\hat{s}_{x}\hat{\bf x}+\hat{s}_{y}\hat{\bf y}
. Also, in the linear response regime,

\hat{s}_{z}({\bf r})=-\sqrt{s^{2}_{e}({\bf r})-(\hat{s}_{x}^{2}({\bf r})+\hat{s}_{y}^{2}({\bf r}))}\approx-s_{e}({\bf r})+(\hat{s}_{x}^{2}({\bf r})+\hat{s}_{y}^{2}({\bf r}))/(2S)=-s_{e}({\bf r})+\tilde{s}(\bf r),

where
\tilde{s}({\bf r})=(\hat{s}_{x}^{2}({\bf r})+\hat{s}_{y}^{2}({\bf r}))/(2S)
relates to the magnitude of spin fluctuation.
The Hamiltonian (
28
) in the linear response regime becomes

\displaystyle\hat{H}=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\dfrac{\hbar}{2S}\int d{\bf r}\left[{\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})-\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})}\right]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\dfrac{\mu_{0}(\gamma\hbar)^{2}}{2}\int d{\bf r}\left[\tilde{s}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})-s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}(\tilde{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right]+\left[\tilde{s}({\bf r})H_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})-s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm ex},z}(\tilde{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\dfrac{\mu_{0}(\gamma\hbar)^{2}}{2}\int d{\bf r}s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}({\bf\hat{s}}_{\perp},{\bf r}),

(34)

where the constant

E_{0}=-\mu_{0}\gamma\hbar\int d{\bf r}H_{0}s_{e}({\bf r})-(\mu_{0}\gamma^{2}\hbar^{2}/2)\int d{\bf r}\left[s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right]
.
The second integral in Eq. (
34
) relates to the fluctuation of the magnetization or the effective magnetic field along the
\hat{\bf z}
-direction, which we demonstrate vanishes:

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}\tilde{s}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\int d{\bf r}d{\bf r}^{\prime}\tilde{s}({\bf r})\nabla_{z}\dfrac{\nabla_{z^{\prime}}{s}_{e}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|}=\int d{\bf r}s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}({\tilde{s}\hat{\bf z}},{\bf r}),

(35)

such that
\int d{\bf r}\left[\tilde{s}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})-s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}(\tilde{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right]=0
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}\tilde{s}({\bf r})H_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})=

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rm ex}\int d{\bf r}\tilde{s}({\bf r})\nabla^{2}s_{e}({\bf r})=\int d{\bf r}s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm ex},z}(\tilde{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r}),

(36)

such that
\int d{\bf r}\left[\tilde{s}({\bf r})H_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})-s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm ex},z}(\tilde{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right]=0
.
The third integral in Eq. (
34
)

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}s_{e}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},z}({\bf\hat{s}}_{\perp},{\bf r})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\int d{\bf r}d{\bf r}^{\prime}\left[s_{e}({\bf r})\nabla_{z}\dfrac{\nabla_{x^{\prime}}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|}+s_{e}({\bf r})\nabla_{z}\dfrac{\nabla_{y^{\prime}}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|}\right]

\displaystyle=\int d{\bf r}\left[s_{x}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},x}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+s_{y}({\bf r})H_{{\rm dip},y}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right]

(37)

vanishes as well since for the uniform saturation magnetization the static transverse dipolar field
H_{{\rm dip},y}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})=0
and
H_{{\rm dip},x}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})=0
. Accordingly, the Hamiltonian (
34
) becomes

\displaystyle\hat{H}=E_{0}+\dfrac{\hbar}{2S}\int d{\bf r}\left({\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})-\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})}\right).

(38)

Since
\hat{m}_{p}
are the magnon operators,
\hat{m}_{p}\propto e^{-i\omega_{p}t}
and
\hat{m}^{\dagger}_{p}\propto e^{i\omega_{p}t}
, where
\omega_{p}
is the eigenfrequency of mode “
p
”. According to Eq. (
26
),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})=

\displaystyle-{i\sqrt{S/2}}\sum_{p}\omega_{p}\left[\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}-\left({\cal A}^{*}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\right],

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})=

\displaystyle-{\sqrt{S/2}}\sum_{p}\omega_{p}\left[\left({\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}-\left({\cal A}^{*}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\right].

(39)

Combining Eqs. (
39
), (
26
) and (
43
), we diagonalize the Hamiltonian (
38
) according to

\displaystyle\hat{H}=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\hbar\int d{\bf r}\sum_{pp^{\prime}}\omega_{p^{\prime}}\left[\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}\hat{m}_{p^{\prime}}^{\dagger}+\left({\cal A}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal B}^{*}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\hat{m}_{p^{\prime}}\right.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.\left({\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})-{\cal A}_{{p}}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}\hat{m}_{p^{\prime}}+\left({\cal B}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal A}_{{p}}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}^{\dagger}_{p}\hat{m}_{p^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{p}\hbar\omega_{p}(\hat{m}_{p}\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}+\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\hat{m}_{p}),

(40)

by the assumption that

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}[{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})]=\delta_{pp^{\prime}},

(41a)

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}[{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})]=0.

(41b)

This implies that the inverse transformation of the operator
\hat{b}({\bf r})
exists: when we express

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{p}

\displaystyle=\int d{\bf r}\left[{\cal A}_{{p}}^{*}({\bf r})\hat{b}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{{p}}({\bf r})\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r})\right],

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\int d{\bf r}\left[{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})\hat{b}^{\dagger}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\hat{b}({\bf r})\right],

(42)

the bosonic characteristic of the operator
\hat{m}_{p}
leads to the other two relations

\displaystyle[\hat{m}_{p},\hat{m}^{\dagger}_{{p}^{\prime}}]=\delta_{pp^{\prime}}\rightarrow\int d{\bf r}[{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})]=\delta_{pp^{\prime}},

\displaystyle[\hat{m}_{p},\hat{m}_{p^{\prime}}]=0\rightarrow\int d{\bf r}[{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})]=0.

(43)

Equations. (
27
) and (
43
) can be regarded as completeness relations that ensure
\hat{m}_{p}
forms a complete set.

{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})
and
{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})
are related to the magnetization amplitudes
{\cal M}_{x,y}^{p}({\bf r})
.
Linearization of the Landau-Lifshitz (LL) equation
\partial_{t}{\bf M}^{p}=-\mu_{0}\gamma{\bf M}^{p}\times(H_{0}\hat{\bf z}+{\bf H}_{\rm ex}+{\bf H}_{\rm dip})

[
48
]
with
{\bf M}^{p}({\bf r},t)=\{{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r})e^{-i\omega_{p}t},{\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})e^{-i\omega_{p}t},M_{s}\}
leads to

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\gamma M_{s}\left(H_{{\rm ex},y}\left[{\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})\hat{\bf y}\right]+{H}_{{\rm dip},y}\left[{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r})\hat{\bf x}+{\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})\hat{\bf y}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=-i\omega_{p}{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r})+\mu_{0}\gamma{\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})\left(H_{0}+{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right),

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\gamma M_{s}\left(H_{{\rm ex},x}\left[{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r})\hat{\bf x}\right]+{H}_{{\rm dip},x}\left[{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r})\hat{\bf x}+{\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})\hat{\bf y}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=i\omega_{p}{\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})-\mu_{0}\gamma{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r})\left(H_{0}+{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})-{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right).

(44)

To obtain the equation of
{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})
and
{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})
, based on Eq. (
33
) we take the commutator

\displaystyle\left[{H}_{{\rm ex},y}(\hat{s}_{y}\hat{\bf y},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},y}(\hat{\bf s}_{\perp},{\bf r}),\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[H_{{\rm dip},y}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+\dfrac{\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})+\mu_{0}\gamma H_{0}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})}{\mu_{0}\gamma^{2}\hbar S}+\dfrac{\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right)}{S},\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\right],

\displaystyle\left[{H}_{{\rm ex},x}(\hat{s}_{x}\hat{\bf x},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm dip},x}(\hat{\bf s}_{\perp},{\bf r}),\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[{H}_{{\rm dip},x}({S}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+\dfrac{-\partial_{t}\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})+\mu_{0}\gamma H_{0}\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})}{\mu_{0}\gamma^{2}\hbar S}+\dfrac{\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})\left({H}_{{\rm dip},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(S\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right)}{S},\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\right],

(45)

leading to

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\gamma M_{s}\left(H_{{\rm ex},y}\left[i({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}))/2\hat{\bf y}\right]+{H}_{{\rm dip},y}\left[({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}))/2\hat{\bf x}+i({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}))/2\hat{\bf y}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=-i\omega_{p}\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right)/2+i\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right)/2\left[H_{0}+{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right],

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\gamma M_{s}\left(H_{{\rm ex},x}\left[({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}))/2\hat{\bf y}\right]+{H}_{{\rm dip},x}\left[({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}))/2\hat{\bf x}+i({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}))/2\hat{\bf y}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=i\omega_{p}i\dfrac{{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})}{2}+\mu_{0}\gamma\dfrac{{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})}{2}\left[H_{0}+{H}_{{\rm dip},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})+{H}_{{\rm ex},z}(M_{s}\hat{\bf z},{\bf r})\right],

(46)

in which
M_{s}=\gamma\hbar S
.
By comparing Eqs. (
44
) and (
46
), we find
{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})
and
{\cal B}_{p}({\bf r})
are related to the mode amplitudes
{\cal M}_{x,y}^{p}({\bf r})
via

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r})=

\displaystyle({1}/{2})\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right),

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})=

\displaystyle({i}/{2})\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right).

(47)

The spin operators can then be expanded by the magnon operators
\{\hat{m}_{p},\hat{m}_{p}^{\dagger}\}
in terms of the magnetization amplitudes
{\cal M}_{x,y}^{p}({\bf r})
as

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{x}({\bf r})

\displaystyle={\sqrt{S/2}}\sum_{p}\left[\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}+{\rm H.c.}\right]=\sqrt{2S}\sum_{p}\left({\cal M}^{p}_{x}({\bf r})\hat{m}_{p}+{\rm H.c.}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{y}({\bf r})

\displaystyle={\sqrt{S/2}}\sum_{p}\left[i\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r})\right)\hat{m}_{p}+{\rm H.c.}\right]=\sqrt{2S}\sum_{p}\left({\cal M}^{p}_{y}({\bf r})\hat{m}_{p}+{\rm H.c.}\right),

(48)

where the amplitudes is normalized according to Eq. (
43
), i.e.,
[
49
,
50
]

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}\left({\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r}){\cal M}_{y}^{{p}^{\prime}*}({\bf r})-{\cal M}_{x}^{{p}^{\prime}*}({\bf r}){\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r})\right)

\displaystyle=\int d{\bf r}\dfrac{i}{2}\left(-{\cal A}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})+{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})\right)=-\dfrac{i}{2}\delta_{pp^{\prime}},

(49a)

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}\left({\cal M}_{y}^{p}({\bf r}){\cal M}_{x}^{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})-{\cal M}_{x}^{p}({\bf r}){\cal M}_{y}^{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})\right)

\displaystyle=\int d{\bf r}\dfrac{i}{2}\left({\cal A}_{p}({\bf r}){\cal B}_{p^{\prime}}^{*}({\bf r})-{\cal B}_{p}^{*}({\bf r}){\cal A}_{p^{\prime}}({\bf r})\right)=0.

(49b)

2.3
Pairing symmetry of Cooper pairs

Since the discovery of superconductivity in Hg when the temperature is cooled below the liquid nitrogen temperature
[
217
,
218
,
219
]
, the investigation of superconductors and their heterostructures has lasted for more than one hundred years. Since 1956, Bardeen, Cooper, and Schrieffer (BCS) proposed the microscopic mechanism for the conventional superconductivity
[
52
,
53
]
, in which the key idea is the formation of Cooper pairs bonded by an even weak attractive potential between two electrons to lower the free energy of the system.
Such potential makes the gapless Fermi sphere unstable, thus favoring a state holding an energy gap at the Fermi energy.
In conventional superconductivity, the attractive potential comes from the electron-phonon interaction
[
53
]
. Afterward, the emergence of the heavy-Fermion superconductivity
[
220
,
221
,
222
,
223
]
, the copper-based superconductivity
[
224
,
225
,
226
,
227
,
228
,
54
,
229
,
230
,
231
]
, the iron-based superconductivity
[
232
,
55
,
233
,
234
,
235
]
challenge the BCS theory as well as the electron-phonon mechanism
[
221
,
222
,
227
,
228
,
54
,
229
,
230
,
231
,
235
]
. Nevertheless, many experimental phenomena can be still well understood via the analysis of the pairing symmetry of Cooper pairs
[
56
,
54
,
55
]
.

Mathematically, the Cooper pairing is described by the anomalous correlation between two electrons
[
56
,
54
,
51
]

F_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}({\bf r}_{1}-{\bf r}_{2},t_{1}-t_{2})=\langle\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma}({\bf r}_{1},t_{1})\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma^{\prime}}({\bf r}_{2},t_{2})\rangle,

(50)

where
\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma}({\bf r},t)
represents the field operator of the electron with spin
\sigma
and the space-time coordinate
({\bf r},t)
.
In terms of the Fourier components in the frequency and momentum space, the anomalous correlation

F_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}({\bf r}_{1}-{\bf r}_{2},t_{1}-t_{2})=f({\bf r},t)S_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}=\Big[\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\frac{d{\bf k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{i{\bf k}\cdot({\bf r}_{1}-{\bf r}_{2})-i\omega(t_{1}-t_{2})}f(\omega,{\bf k})\Big]S_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}},

(51)

where
f({\bf r},t)
and
S_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}
are the “wavefunction” of the Cooper pairs in the real and spin spaces
[
56
,
51
]
,
d
is the dimension,
f(\omega,{\bf k})
is the Fourier components
(\omega,{\bf k})
of the wavefunction.
Since any Fermion obeys the Fermi-Dirac statistics, exchanging the space, time, and spin indexes of the two field operators, the anomalous correlation function (
50
) changes sign. Such an operation of exchanging the space and time is equivalent to replacing
-{\bf k}
and
-\omega
in Eq. (
51
). Thus, depending on the spin singlet and triplet in the spin wavefunction
S_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}
, we can classify the Cooper pairing in terms of the parity dependence on the frequency and momentum in
f(\omega,{\bf k})

[
56
,
54
,
55
,
236
,
15
]
.

When the spin wavefunction
S_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}
is in its spin singlet state, exchanging the spin index, the spin wavefunction changes sign. So
f(\omega,{\bf k})=f(-\omega,-{\bf k})
remains unchanged in the real-space wavefunction by the Fermi-Dirac statistics.
Accordingly,
f(\omega,{\bf k})
is either odd in the frequency and momentum or even in the frequency and momentum. Furthermore, when the Cooper pairing function
f(\omega,{\bf k})
is even in the momentum, the associated superconductivity is in the
\{s,d,...\}
waves; when
f(\omega,{\bf k})
is odd in the momentum, it lies in the
\{p,f,...\}
waves.
On the other hand, when the spin wavefunction
S_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}
is in the spin-triplet state, exchanging the spin index, the spin wavefunction remains unchanged. According to the Fermi-Dirac statistics,

f(\omega,{\bf k})=-f(-\omega,-{\bf k})
. Thus,
f(\omega,{\bf k})
is either even in the frequency and odd in the momentum or is odd in the frequency and even in the momentum. Table
1
summarizes the various Cooper pairing
[
236
]
.

Table 1:
Classification of Cooper pairings
f(\omega,{\bf k})
in the frequency and momentum spaces according to the spin singlet and triplet pairings.

Spin

Frequency

Momentum

Singlet (odd)

\uparrow\downarrow-\downarrow\uparrow

Even

Even

Odd

Odd

\uparrow\uparrow~~~~~~\downarrow\downarrow

Triplet (even)

\uparrow\downarrow+\downarrow\uparrow

Even

Odd

Odd

Even

When the Cooper pairing (
50
) is known, in the framework of the weak-coupling theory, the Landau order parameter is calculated according to the gap equation
[
56
,
54
,
51
]

\Delta_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}({\bf k})=\sum_{{\bf k}^{\prime},\omega,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}V_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}({\bf k},{\bf k}^{\prime})F_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}({\bf k}^{\prime},\omega),

(52)

where
V_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}({\bf k},{\bf k}^{\prime})
is the pairing potential of the superconductivity. In the absence of the SOC it can be factorized as
V_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}({\bf k},{\bf k}^{\prime})=V({\bf k},{\bf k}^{\prime})\Gamma_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
. In conventional singlet
s
-wave superconductivity
V({\bf k},{\bf k}^{\prime})
is taken as a constant
V_{0}
,
\Gamma_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}=(1/2)(i\sigma_{y})_{\sigma\sigma}(-i\sigma_{y})_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
and the summation over
\omega
is restricted by the Debye frequency
\Omega_{D}
.
We note that the symmetry of the superconducting order parameter does not always follow from that of the superconducting pairing since the symmetry of the pairing potential is also essential according to the gap equation (
52
)
[
56
]
.
For example, when the triplet, even-frequency,
p
-wave pairing in the superconductor exists, the Cooper pairing is odd in momentum according to Table.
1
.
For the singlet
s
-channel with constant pairing potential
V_{0}
and
\Gamma_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}=(1/2)(i\sigma_{y})_{\sigma\sigma}(-i\sigma_{y})_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
, according to the gap equation (
52
), there exists no triplet order parameter. From this point of view, one concludes that the realization of the triplet order parameter requires an exceptional pairing potential
[
56
]
.

For example, we provide a detailed explanation of triplet superconductivity. Theoretically, there are three levels to describe the triplet superconductivity from the micro to macroscopic point of view
[
51
,
56
]
. Firstly, at the most microscopic level, the Cooper pairing can characterize the triplet superconductivity, which can be represented as a
2\times 2
matrix
[{\bf f}(\omega,{\bf k}^{\prime})\cdot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}]i\sigma_{y}
in the spin space, where the
{\bf f}
vector depends on both the momentum
{\bf k}^{\prime}
and the frequency
\omega
.
Secondly, when the dependence on the frequency in the Cooper pairing is integrated out in the integral (
51
), the anomalous correlation is described by the
{\bf l}
vector:
[{\bf l}({\bf k}^{\prime})\cdot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}]i\sigma_{y}
. Finally, at the most macroscopic level, when the dependence on the momentum in the anomalous correlation is further integrated out with the momentum dependence of the pairing potential in the triplet channel
V_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}=(1/2)V({\bf k},{\bf k}^{\prime})(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}\sigma_{y})_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(-i\sigma_{y}\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
in the gap equation (
52
), we obtain the Landau superconducting order parameter, which is described by the
{\bf d}
vector:
\hat{\Delta}=[{\bf d}({\bf k})\cdot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}]i\sigma_{y}
.

The triplet superconductivity can usually be divided into the unitary and non-unitary classes using the
{\bf d}
vector of the order parameter
[
51
,
56
]
. In this point of view, the order-parameter matrix
\hat{\Delta}
in the spin space is treated as a wave function of the Cooper pairs. The expectation of the spin polarization of the triplet Cooper pairs is then calculated by
[
51
,
56
]

{\rm tr}[\hat{\Delta}^{\dagger}\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\hat{\Delta}]\propto i{\bf d}\times{\bf d}^{*}.

(53)

Accordingly, when
i{\bf d}\times{\bf d}^{*}=0
, the associated triplet Cooper pairs have no net spin polarization, which satisfies
{\bf d}^{*}={\bf d}
. Such triplet superconductivity is called unitary triplet superconductivity; otherwise, it is called non-unitary triplet superconductivity
[
51
,
56
]
.
Several possible systems are candidates of triplet superconductivity, including the superfluid
3
He
[
51
,
237
,
238
,
239
,
240
,
241
]
and unconventional superconductor Sr
2
RuO
4

[
238
,
239
,
240
,
51
,
242
,
243
,
244
,
245
,
246
,
247
,
248
,
249
]
. The triplet Cooper pairing may also appear in conventional superconductors
[
250
,
251
,
252
,
253
,
254
,
255
]
with interface SOC, non-centrosymmetric superconductor
[
56
,
256
,
76
]
, and the conventional superconductor-ferromagnet interface
[
92
,
59
,
60
,
93
,
257
,
258
,
259
,
14
,
15
]
.

2.4
Dynamics of superconductors

According to the BCS theory, the conventional superconductivity may be well described by the Bogoliubov de-Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian under the mean-field approximation
[
57
,
58
]

\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{0}+\hat{H}_{\rm int}+\hat{H}_{\rm im}.

(54)

With the field operator
\hat{\Psi}=(\hat{\psi}_{\uparrow},\hat{\psi}_{\downarrow}^{\dagger})^{T}
under the Nambu representation, the kinetic energy

\hat{H}_{0}=\int d{\bf r}\hat{\Psi}^{\dagger}({\bf r})\Big(\frac{\displaystyle{\bf p}^{2}}{\displaystyle 2m}-\mu\Big)\tau_{3}\hat{\Psi}({\bf r}),

(55)

where
m
and
\mu
are, respectively, the mass of the electrons and the chemical potential of the system;
\tau_{3}={\rm diag}(1,-1)
is the metric in the Nambu representation. The superconducting order parameter
\Delta({\bf r})=\lambda\langle\hat{\psi}_{\downarrow}({\bf r})\hat{\psi}_{\uparrow}({\bf r})\rangle
under the mean-field approximation
[
57
,
58
]
is responsible for the superconductivity phenomena,
where
\lambda<0
is the electron-electron effective attractive potential, with which the pairing Hamiltonian

\hat{H}_{\rm int}=\int d{\bf r}\hat{\Psi}^{\dagger}({\bf r})\tilde{\Delta}({\bf r})\tau_{3}\hat{\Psi}({\bf r}),

(56)

in which
\tilde{\Delta}({\bf r})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-\Delta({\bf r})\\
\Delta^{*}({\bf r})&0\end{array}\right)
.
The electron-impurity interaction Hamiltonian

\hat{H}_{\rm im}=\int d{\bf r}\hat{\Psi}^{\dagger}({\bf r})U({\bf r})\tau_{3}\hat{\Psi}({\bf r}),

(57)

where
U({\bf r})
is the electron-impurity interaction potential. Starting from the Heisenberg equation of motion, we can derive the BdG equation for the field operator
\hat{\Psi}
as

i\partial_{t}\hat{\Psi}=\hat{H}\hat{\Psi}.

(58)

The dynamics of conventional superconducting metals is described by the dynamic equation based on the quasi-classical approximation
[
59
,
92
,
60
,
93
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
2
,
66
,
67
,
94
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
. The quasi-classical approximation may be understood in terms of the two-particle Green’s function
G({\bf r}_{1},{\bf r}_{2};t_{1},t_{2})
. Making the Fourier transformation
{\bf r}_{1}-{\bf r}_{2}\to{\bf p}
and
t_{1}-t_{2}\to E
, it is convenient to work further with the Green’s function
G({\bf p},{\bf R};E,T)
, where
{\bf R}=({\bf r}_{1}+{\bf r}_{2})/2
is the center-of-mass coordinate and
T=(t_{1}+t_{2})/2
is the center-of-mass time.
The dependence on the momentum
k
can be further decomposed into the dependence on the modulus value of the momentum or the normal-state energy
\xi_{{\bf p}}
and the direction of the momentum
\hat{\bf p}={\bf p}/|{\bf p}|
.
The quasi-classical approximation is generally applied to the case that the system has a large Fermi surface, such that
\Delta/E_{F}\ll 1
, where
E_{F}
is the corresponding Fermi energy.
In this case, the main superconductivity-related physics occurs near the Fermi surface
[
59
,
92
,
60
,
93
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
2
,
66
,
67
,
94
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
.
In this situation, the dependence of the Green’s function on
\xi_{{\bf k}}
may be integrated out, while its dependence on
\hat{\bf p}
,
{\bf R}
,
E
and
T
should be dealt with explicitly.
It is worth noting that for systems with small Fermi surfaces, the quasi-classical approximation may not be applicable, as all electrons participate in the dynamics. In this situation, we may alternatively choose the quasiparticle approximation
[
260
,
261
]
.
In the quasiparticle approximation, the information about the frequency
E
in the Green’s function is integrated out, while the Green’s function explicitly depends on the momentum, the center-of-mass coordinates, and the center-of-mass time
[
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
90
,
91
]
. In general, the quasiparticle approximation can only be applied when the perturbation to the system’s superconducting order parameter is small, and then the quasiparticle energy spectrum is well defined.

Using the quasi-classical approximation
[
59
,
92
,
60
,
93
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
2
,
66
,
67
,
94
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
, we can set up the Eilenberger equation
[
62
,
67
]
, the Usadel equation
[
66
]
, and the macroscopic Ginzburg-Landau equation
[
79
]
that are commonly used for the superconductor dynamics from the fully microscopic Gor’kov equation
[
57
,
58
]
. In general, from the most microscopic level, the Gor’kov equation can be used as a starting point for studying the dynamics of superconductors. However, the Gor’kov equation involves calculating all the degrees of freedom of the Green’s function in the dynamic process, which is a challenging calculation.
When the Fermi energy
E_{F}
of the metal is much larger than the superconducting order parameter
\Delta
, the quasi-classical approximation can be applied to simplify the Gor’kov equation. Such a simplified equation is called the Eilenberger equation
[
62
,
67
]
. Furthermore, the Eilenberger equation can be reduced to the Usadel equation in the dirty sample limit
[
66
]
.
When the temperature
T
is close to the superconducting transition temperature
T_{c}
, the superconducting order parameter of the system becomes small. In this situation, the Gor’kov and Eilenberger equations can be further reduced to the macroscopic Ginzburg-Landau equation about the superconducting order parameter
[
79
]
. In this part, we elaborate on the derivation of the Gor’kov
[
57
,
58
]
, Eilenberger
[
62
,
67
]
, Usadel
[
66
]
, and Ginzburg-Landau equations
[
79
]
in conventional superconducting metals based on the Keldysh Green’s function method
[
71
,
73
,
74
]
.

2.4.1
Gor’kov equation

The Gor’kov equation
[
57
,
58
]
is governed by the nonequilibrium contour Green’s function
[
71
,
73
,
74
]
under the Hamiltonian (
54
).
Under the Nambu representation, the contour Green’s function of the system is defined as
[
71
]

\displaystyle\underline{\tilde{G}}_{12}=-i\tau_{3}\langle T_{c}\hat{\Psi}_{1}\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\dagger}\rangle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\tilde{G}_{12}^{--}&\tilde{G}_{12}^{-+}\\
\tilde{G}_{12}^{+-}&\tilde{G}_{12}^{++}\end{array}\right),

which is a
4\times 4
matrix.
In Eq. (
2.4.1
), “1” and “2” indicate the spatiotemporal coordinate points
(t_{1},{\bf r}_{1})
and
(t_{2},{\bf r}_{2})
. The components
\tilde{G}_{12}^{--}=-i\tau_{3}\langle T\hat{\Psi}_{1}\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\dagger}\rangle
,
\tilde{G}_{12}^{-+}=i\tau_{3}\langle\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{\Psi}_{1}\rangle
,

\tilde{G}_{12}^{+-}=-i\tau_{3}\langle\hat{\Psi}_{1}\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\dagger}\rangle
, and
\tilde{G}_{12}^{++}=-i\tau_{3}\langle\tilde{T}\hat{\Psi}_{1}\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\dagger}\rangle
, where
T
and
\tilde{T}
indicate the chronological and inverse chronological products of operators.
In the Keldysh-space
[
71
]
, the non-equilibrium Green’s function (
2.4.1
) is transformed to

\underline{\tilde{G}}_{12}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\tilde{G}_{12}^{R}&\tilde{G}_{12}^{K}\\
0&\tilde{G}_{12}^{A}\end{array}\right),

(61)

in which

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{12}^{R}=G_{12}^{--}-G_{12}^{-+}=G_{12}^{+-}-G_{12}^{++},

(62a)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{12}^{A}=G_{12}^{--}-G_{12}^{+-}=G_{12}^{-+}-G_{12}^{++},

(62b)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{12}^{K}=G_{12}^{++}+G_{12}^{--}=G_{12}^{+-}+G_{12}^{-+}.

(62c)

Furthermore, following the BdG equation (
58
), the Green’s function (
61
) obeys the Gor’kov equation
[
57
,
58
]

\displaystyle\int dx_{3}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}}_{13}\underline{\tilde{G}}_{32}=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})I_{4\times 4}+\int dx_{3}\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}_{13}\underline{\tilde{G}}_{32},

(63a)

\displaystyle\int dx_{3}\underline{\tilde{G}}_{13}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}}_{32}=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})I_{4\times 4}+\int dx_{3}\underline{\tilde{G}}_{13}\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}_{32},

(63b)

in which

\displaystyle\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}}_{13}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}i\frac{\displaystyle\partial}{\displaystyle\partial t_{1}}\tau_{3}+\frac{\displaystyle\partial_{1}^{2}}{\displaystyle 2m}+\mu-\tilde{\Delta}_{1}&0\\
0&i\frac{\displaystyle\partial}{\displaystyle\partial t_{1}}\tau_{3}+\frac{\displaystyle\partial_{1}^{2}}{\displaystyle 2m}+\mu-\tilde{\Delta}_{1}\end{array}\right)\delta(x_{1}-x_{3}),

\displaystyle\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}}_{32}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-i\frac{\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\partial}}}{\displaystyle\partial t_{2}}\tau_{3}+\frac{\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\partial_{2}^{2}}}}{\displaystyle 2m}+\mu-\tilde{\Delta}_{2}&0\\
0&-i\frac{\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\partial}}}{\displaystyle\partial t_{2}}\tau_{3}+\frac{\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\displaystyle\partial_{2}^{2}}}}{\displaystyle 2m}+\mu-\tilde{\Delta}_{2}\end{array}\right)\delta(x_{3}-x_{2}),

and

{\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}}_{13}=U_{1}\delta(x_{1}-x_{3})\underline{\tilde{G}}_{13}

(65)

is the self-energy contributed by the electron-impurity interaction
[
262
]
.

The Gor’kov equation (
63a
) and (
63b
) contain all the microscopic information of the system, which is then challenging to solve universally.
Fortunately, starting from the Gor’kov equation, the kinetic equations obeyed by the quasiparticles can be obtained under the quasiparticle approximation
[
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
90
,
91
]
. On the other hand, under the quasi-classical approximation
[
71
,
73
,
74
]
, the Eilenberger equation for the dynamics of superconducting order parameter can be derived
[
62
,
67
]
.

2.4.2
Eilenberger equation

In this part, we set up the Eilenberger equation
[
62
,
67
]
from the Gor’kov equation [(
63a
) and (
63b
)]. We first simplify the notation, i.e.,
\int dx_{3}A_{13}B_{32}=(A\otimes B)(1,2)
. With such notation, the Gor’kov equation (
63a
) and (
63b
) are expressed as
[
71
,
73
,
74
]

\displaystyle(\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}})(1,2)=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})+(\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}})(1,2),

(66a)

\displaystyle(\underline{\tilde{G}}\otimes\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}})(1,2)=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})+(\underline{\tilde{G}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}})(1,2).

(66b)

Taking the difference of Eqs. (
66a
) and (
66b
) leads to

(\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}})(1,2)-(\underline{\tilde{G}}\otimes\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftarrow}}{{\mathcal{G}}}})(1,2)=[\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(1,2),

(67)

where
[\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(1,2)\equiv(\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}})(1,2)-(\underline{\tilde{G}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}})(1,2)
.
With the center-of-mass coordinates
T=(t_{1}+t_{2})/2
and
{\bf R}=({\bf R}_{1}+{\bf R}_{2})/2
and the relative coordinates
t=t_{1}-t_{2}
and
{\bf r}={\bf r}_{1}-{\bf r}_{2}
, Eq. (
67
) is alternatively expressed as

i\Big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial T}+\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big)\hat{\tau}_{3}\underline{\tilde{G}}_{12}+i\Big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial T}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big)\underline{\tilde{G}}_{12}\hat{\tau}_{3}+\frac{1}{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf r}}\underline{\tilde{G}}_{12}+[\hat{\Delta}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(1,2)=[\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}\otimes\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(1,2),

(68)

where
\hat{\tau}_{3}={\rm diag}(\tau_{3},\tau_{3})
and
\hat{\Delta}={\rm diag}(-\tilde{\Delta},-\tilde{\Delta})
.
In the Fourier space, the Green’s function

\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)=\int dtd{\bf r}e^{iEt-i{\bf p}\cdot{\bf r}}\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf r},t).

(69)

Accordingly, Eq. (
68
) is written in the Fourier space as

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial T}\Big\{\hat{\tau}_{3},\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\Big\}+\Big[E\hat{\tau}_{3},\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\Big]+\frac{i{\bf p}}{m}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)+[\hat{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathrm{full}}\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(X,p)

\displaystyle=[\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}\otimes_{\mathrm{full}}\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(X,p).

(70)

In Eq. (
70
),
\{\ ,\ \}
and
[\ ,\ ]
indicate the anticommutator and commutator, respectively, and

(A\otimes_{\mathrm{full}}B)(X,p)=\int dxe^{-ipx}\int dx_{3}A(1,3)B(3,2)=\exp[i(\partial_{X}^{A}\partial_{p}^{B}-\partial_{p}^{A}\partial_{X}^{B})/2]A(X,p)B(X,p),

(71)

where
X\equiv(T,{\bf R})
,
p\equiv(E,{\bf p})
,
px\equiv-Et+{\bf p}\cdot{\bf r}
,
\partial_{X}\equiv(-\partial_{T},\nabla_{\mathbf{R}})
,
\partial_{p}\equiv(-\partial_{E},\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})
, and
\partial_{X}^{A}\partial_{p}^{B}\equiv-\partial_{T}^{A}\partial_{E}^{B}+\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}^{A}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}}^{B}
.

In Eq. (
70
), the last term on the left-hand side and the term on the right-hand side are still complicated. They can be simplified by exploiting the gradient expansion over the center-of-mass coordinate
\mathbf{R}
and the momentum
\mathbf{p}

[
71
,
73
,
74
]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle(A\otimes_{\mathrm{full}}B)(X,p)

\displaystyle=A(X,p)\otimes B(X,p)+\frac{i}{2}\left(\partial_{\mathbf{R}}^{A}\partial_{\mathbf{p}}^{B}-\partial_{\mathbf{p}}^{A}\partial_{\mathbf{R}}^{B}\right)A(X,p)\otimes B(X,p)+...

\displaystyle\approx A(X,p)\otimes B(X,p),

(72)

where

\displaystyle A(X,p)\otimes B(X,p)=\exp[-i(\partial_{T}^{A}\partial_{E}^{B}-\partial_{E}^{A}\partial_{T}^{B})/2]A(X,p)B(X,p).

(73)

All gradient terms, which are disregarded in Eq. (
72
), contain small factor
1/(\xi_{S}p_{F})\sim\Delta/E_{F}
in different powers
[
71
,
263
]
and, consequently, are small as compared to the leading order term
A(X,p)\otimes B(X,p)
. Here
\xi_{S}
is the superconducting coherence length. At the same time, in general non-stationary situation the gradient terms containing derivatives with respect to the center-of-mass time and energy cannot be disregarded, see for example, Refs.
[
95
,
192
,
193
]
.

Retaining the lowest order in the spatial and momentum gradient expansion (
72
) yields

\displaystyle[\hat{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathrm{full}}\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(X,p)\approx\big[\hat{\Delta}({\bf R},T),\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\big]_{\otimes},

(74a)

\displaystyle[\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}\otimes_{\mathrm{full}}\underline{\tilde{G}}]_{-}(X,p)\approx\big[\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E),\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\big]_{\otimes},

(74b)

where
[A,B]_{\otimes}=A\otimes B-B\otimes A
. In Eq. (
74b
), the self-energy

\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)
contributed by the electron-impurity interaction can be calculated by the Feynman diagram in the momentum space. After averaging the position of the impurity in the sample
[
71
,
73
,
74
,
262
]
, we obtain

\underline{\tilde{\Sigma}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)=\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int\frac{d\Omega_{{\bf p}^{\prime}}}{4\pi}\int d\xi_{{\bf p}^{\prime}}\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p}^{\prime},E),

(75)

where
\tau
is the momentum relaxation time due to the electron-impurity scattering and
\Omega_{{\bf p}}
is the solid angle. Substituting Eqs. (
74a
), (
74b
) and (
75
) into (
68
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3}+\hat{\Delta}({\bf R},T),\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\big]_{\otimes}+\frac{i{\bf p}}{m}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int\frac{d\Omega_{{\bf p}^{\prime}}}{4\pi}\int d\xi_{{\bf p}^{\prime}}\big[\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p}^{\prime},E),\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\big]_{\otimes}=0,

(76)

where it is taken into account that
(i/2)\partial_{T}\big\{\hat{\tau}_{3},\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\big\}+\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3},\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\big]=\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3},\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E)\big]_{\otimes}
. It is worth noting that Eq. (
76
) has been significantly simplified compared to the Gor’kov equation. Considering the quasi-classical approximation
[
71
,
73
,
74
]
, Eq. (
76
) can be further simplified to obtain the Eilenberger equation
[
62
,
67
]
.

When the metallic system has a large Fermi energy, the main physics occurs near the Fermi surface.
In this situation, the physical quantities of the system may mainly depend on the direction of the momentum
{\bf p}
, while the modulus of the momentum can be simply set to the Fermi momentum
p_{F}
.
Correspondingly, the dependence on the momentum modulus can be integrated out, with which the quasi-classical Green’s function is defined as
[
71
,
73
,
74
]

\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)=\frac{i}{\pi}\int d\xi_{\bf p}\underline{\tilde{G}}({\bf R},T;{\bf p},E),

(77)

where
\hat{\bf p}\equiv{\bf p}/|{\bf p}|
is the direction of the momentum.
With Eq. (
76
), we obtain the Eilenberger equation satisfied by the quasi-classical Green’s function
[
62
,
67
]

\displaystyle\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3}+\hat{\Delta}({\bf R}),\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)\big]_{\otimes}+i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)+\Big[\frac{i}{2\tau}\langle\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}}^{\prime},E)\rangle,\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)\Big]_{\otimes}=0,

(78)

where
\langle\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}}^{\prime},E)\rangle=1/(4\pi)\int d\Omega_{{\bf p}^{\prime}}\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}}^{\prime},E)
and
{\bf v}_{F}\equiv{\bf p}_{F}/m
is the Fermi velocity.
In the steady state, the explicit dependence of all quantities on
T
is absent and the
\otimes
-product is just the conventional product. Then Eq. (
78
) is reduced to

\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3}+\hat{\Delta}({\bf R}),\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R};\hat{{\bf p}},E)\big]+i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R};\hat{{\bf p}},E)+\Big[\frac{i}{2\tau}\langle\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R};\hat{{\bf p}}^{\prime},E)\rangle,\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R};\hat{{\bf p}},E)\Big]=0.

(79)

In contrast to the original Gor’kov equation (
63a
)-(
63b
) the quasiclassical equation (
79
) is homogeneous, and
hence in some sense it leaves the “norm” of
\underline{\tilde{g}}
undetermined. Eilenberger
[
62
]

discovered the missing “normalization condition” to be

\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)\otimes\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)=I_{4\times 4}.

(80)

However, there are still redundant solutions in the system that need to be excluded with the help of asymptotic conditions appropriate to the given problem. Because of the need to solve the Eilenberger equation for a lot of quasiparticle trajectories
\hat{{\bf p}}
, the process is still relatively difficult. Usadel considered further simplifying the problem in the strong scattering limit, when the quasiclassical Green’s function
\underline{\tilde{g}}
tends to be isotropic in the momentum space, and derived the Usadel equation
[
66
]
. Usadel equation
[
66
]
is widely used to solve the problem of superconducting mesoscopics in stationary and non-stationary situations
[
59
,
92
,
60
,
93
,
94
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
,
95
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
.

2.4.3
Usadel equation

In the strong scattering limit, considering the Green’s function tends to be isotropic in the momentum space, we are allowed to divide the quasi-classical Green’s function (
77
) into the isotropic and small anisotropic components in the momentum space. That is, we perform the angle expansion over the Green’s function and retain the lowest orders with

\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)=\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)+\hat{\bf p}\cdot\underline{\tilde{\bf g}}_{p}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E),

(81)

where
\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\equiv\int d{\Omega}_{\bf p}/(4\pi)\underline{\tilde{g}}({\bf R},T;\hat{{\bf p}},E)
is the angle averaged quasi-classical Green’s function, and the vector
\underline{\tilde{\bf g}}_{p}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)=\underline{\tilde{g}}_{p,x}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\hat{\bf x}+\underline{\tilde{g}}_{p,y}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\hat{\bf y}+\underline{\tilde{g}}_{p,z}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\hat{\bf z}
.
In particular,
\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)
and
\underline{\tilde{\bf g}}_{p}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)

no longer depend on the direction of the momentum. We further assume that the system slightly deviates from the isotropic case, so

|\underline{\tilde{\bf g}}_{p}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)|\ll|\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)|
.
Substituting Eq. (
81
) into the Eilenberger equation (
79
), we find the even orders of
\hat{\bf p}
obey

\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3}+\hat{\Delta}({\bf R}),\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\big]_{\otimes}+iv_{F}\hat{{\bf p}}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}\big[\hat{\bf p}\cdot\underline{\tilde{\bf g}}_{p}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\big]=0;

(82)

while the odd orders of
\hat{\bf p}
obey

\displaystyle\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3}+\hat{\Delta}({\bf R}),\hat{\bf p}\cdot\underline{\tilde{\bf g}}_{p}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\big]_{\otimes}+iv_{F}\hat{{\bf p}}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)+\frac{i}{2\tau}\big[\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E),\hat{\bf p}\cdot\underline{\tilde{\bf g}}_{p}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\big]_{\otimes}=0.

(83)

With further considering the normalization condition (
80
), we obtain

\displaystyle\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\otimes\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)=1,

(84a)

\displaystyle\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\otimes\underline{\tilde{g}}_{p,\eta}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)+\underline{\tilde{g}}_{p,\eta}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\otimes\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)=0,

(84b)

where
\eta=\{x,y,z\}
. Then with Eqs. (
84a
) and (
84b
), disregarding the first term in Eq. (
83
), we find

v_{F}\tau\frac{\partial}{\partial{R_{\eta}}}\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)+\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\otimes\underline{\tilde{g}}_{p,\eta}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)=0.

(85)

We multiply
\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)
on both sides of Eq. (
85
), substitute it into Eq. (
82
), and find

\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3}+\hat{\Delta}({\bf R}),\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\big]_{\otimes}-iv_{F}^{2}\tau\hat{{\bf p}}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\big[\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\otimes\hat{\bf p}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\big]=0.

(86)

Finally, by taking the angle average over Eq. (
86
), we arrive at the Usadel equation
[
66
]

\big[E\hat{\tau}_{3}+\hat{\Delta}({\bf R}),\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\big]_{\otimes}-\frac{iv_{F}^{2}\tau}{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\cdot\Big[\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\underline{\tilde{g}}_{s}({\bf R},T;p_{F},E)\Big]_{\otimes}=0.

(87)

2.4.4
Ginzburg-Landau equation

From a self-consistent point of view, when the temperature is close to the superconducting transition temperature and the superconducting order parameter is small, the Gor’kov equation
[
57
,
58
]
and the Eilenberger equation
[
62
,
67
]
should reduce to the Ginzburg-Landau equation
[
79
]
, which describes the dynamics of the macroscopic superconducting order parameter.

Before specifically deriving the Ginzburg-Landau equation, we consider the equations obeyed by the components of the quasi-classical Green’s function

\underline{\tilde{g}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\tilde{g}_{R}&\tilde{g}_{K}\\
0&\tilde{g}_{A}\end{array}\right)
. Here, we only consider the clean limit. Starting from the Eilenberger equation (
79
), we obtain the equation satisfied by the components of the quasi-classical Green’s function

\big[E\tau_{3}-\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}\big]+i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}=0.

(88)

In addition, the normalization condition (
80
) provides another set of equations satisfied by the components of the quasi-classical Green’s function:

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{R,A}\tilde{g}_{R,A}=I_{2\times 2},

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{R}\tilde{g}_{K}+\tilde{g}_{K}\tilde{g}_{A}=0.

(89)

The components
\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}
of the quasi-classical Green’s function can be connected by the distribution function

\tilde{h}

[
71
,
73
,
74
]
, which is a
2\times 2
matrix, i.e.,

\tilde{g}_{K}=\tilde{g}_{R}\tilde{h}-\tilde{h}\tilde{g}_{A}.

(90)

Equation (
90
) automatically satisfies the second of Eqs. (
89
). Such a distribution function
\tilde{h}
can always be set to be diagonal
[
71
,
73
,
74
]
, as briefly demonstrated below. Making use of the first of Eq. (
89
) it is easily seen that the transformation
\tilde{h}\rightarrow\tilde{h}+\tilde{h}^{\prime}=\tilde{h}+\tilde{g}_{R}\tilde{x}+\tilde{x}\tilde{g}_{A}
, where
\tilde{x}
is an arbitrary matrix function, leaves the expression (
90
) unchanged.
In this way, we can always choose a special
\tilde{x}
to set
\tilde{h}
to be diagonal.
In particular, in the equilibrium state,
\tilde{h}
is proportional to the identity matrix
[
71
,
73
,
74
]

\tilde{h}_{e}=1-2f_{0}(\beta E)=\tanh(\beta E/2),

(91)

where
f_{0}(x)\equiv 1/(e^{x}+1)
is the Fermi-Dirac distribution function.
Indeed,
\tilde{h}
has the physical implication of a distribution function. In the non-equilibrium situation,
\tilde{h}

can be solved by the kinetic equations.
In addition, by definition, the advanced and retarded quasi-classical Green’s functions satisfy the relation

\tilde{g}_{A}=-\tau_{3}\tilde{g}_{R}\tau_{3}

[
71
,
73
,
74
]
.
Thereby, starting from
\tilde{g}_{R}
and the distribution function
\tilde{h}
, we can find
\tilde{g}_{K}
via (
90
). With
\tilde{g}_{K}
, we can solve the superconducting order parameter
[
71
,
73
,
74
]

\Delta({\bf R})=\frac{1}{8}|\lambda|N_{0}\int\mbox{Tr}\big[(\tau_{1}-i\tau_{2})\tilde{g}_{K}({\bf R};\hat{\bf p},E)\big]\frac{d\Omega_{\bf p}}{4\pi}\frac{dE}{2\pi},

(92)

where
N_{0}
is the density of states of the electrons at the Fermi surface in the normal state, and
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are the
\hat{\bf x}
and
\hat{\bf y}
components of the Pauli matrix.

Near the superconducting transition temperature, the superconducting order parameter is small. The components of the quasi-classical Green’s function can be expanded by the superconducting order parameter and its spatial gradients
[
94
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
73
]
as

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}=\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}^{(0)}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}^{(n)},

(93)

where
\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}^{(0)}
is determined by its equilibrium and spatially homogeneous superconducting state and, when
n\geq 1
,
\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}^{(n)}\propto\Delta^{n}
and contains spatial derivatives of the superconducting order parameter
\Delta
.

We first solve the quasi-classical Green’s function
\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)}

[
71
,
73
,
74
]
. Neglecting spatial gradients of the superconducting order parameter from Eq. (
88
) and the normalization condition (
80
),
\tilde{g}^{(0)}_{R}
obeys

\displaystyle\big[E\tau_{3}-\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{g}^{(0)}_{R}\big]=0,

(94a)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{(0)}_{R}\tilde{g}^{(0)}_{R}=I_{2\times 2},

(94b)

from which we find
\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{E^{2}-|\Delta|^{2}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}E&\Delta\\
-\Delta^{*}&-E\end{array}\right)

[
71
,
73
,
74
]
.
Accordingly, from Eqs. (
90
) and (
91
), we obtain

\tilde{g}_{K}^{(0)}=2\tanh\Big(\frac{\beta E}{2}\Big)\frac{1}{\sqrt{E^{2}-|\Delta|^{2}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}E&\Delta\\
-\Delta^{*}&-E\end{array}\right).

(95)

Substituting Eq. (
95
) into the gap equation (
92
) yields

1=N_{0}\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dE\tanh\Big(\frac{\beta E}{2}\Big)\frac{1}{\sqrt{(E+i0^{+})^{2}-\Delta^{2}}}=\frac{2\pi}{\beta}N_{0}|\lambda|\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{n}^{2}+\Delta^{2}}},

(96)

where
\beta=1/(k_{B}T)
and
\omega_{n}=(2n+1)\pi/\beta
is the Matsubara frequency.
It is crucial to note that the summation here cannot be taken to infinity since a natural truncated energy exists, i.e., the Debye frequency
\Omega_{\rm D}
.
We then define a truncated integer
M_{0}
, governed by
2\pi(M_{0}+1/2)/\beta=k_{B}\Omega_{\rm D}
. Thus,
M_{0}(T)\approx\Omega_{D}/(2\pi T)
is dependent on the temperature. In this way, we can derive the critical temperature
T_{c}
for superconductivity based on the gap equation (
96
). At
T_{c}
,
\Delta=0
; thus by Eq. (
96
) we obtain

\frac{1}{N_{0}|\lambda|}=\sum_{n=0}^{M_{0}={\Omega_{D}}/({2\pi T_{c}})}\frac{2}{2n+1}\approx\gamma+\ln\big(\frac{\Omega_D}{2\pi T_c}\big)+2\ln 2,

(97)

where
\gamma=\gamma_{k\rightarrow\infty}\equiv 1+1/2+1/3+...+1/k-\ln k
is the Euler constant
[
264
]
.
According to Eq. (
97
), the superconducting transition temperature
T_{c}=(2\Omega_{D}/\pi)e^{\gamma}e^{-1/(N_{0}|\lambda|)}

[
51
]
.

We proceed to derive
\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}^{(n\geq 1)}
, taking into account the diffusion of the fluctuation.
We first derive the first-order correction
\tilde{g}_{R,A,K}^{(1)}
.
Expressing the retarded Green’s function

\tilde{g}_{R}=\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)}+\tilde{g}_{R}^{(1)}
, from Eqs. (
88
)-(
89
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big[E\tau_{3}-\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{g}_{R}^{(1)}\big]=-i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)},

(98a)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)}\tilde{g}_{R}^{(1)}+\tilde{g}_{R}^{(1)}\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)}=0,

(98b)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)}\tilde{g}_{R}^{(0)}=1.

(98c)

The components in
\tilde{g}_{R}^{(1)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}g_{R,11}^{(1)}&g_{R,12}^{(1)}\\
g_{R,21}^{(1)}&g_{R,22}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)
can be solved from Eqs. (
98a
)-(
98c
). Specifically,

g_{R,11}^{(1)}=\frac{\left(i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}g_{R,12}^{(0)}\right)g_{R,21}^{(0)}-\left(i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}g_{R,21}^{(0)}\right)g_{R,11}^{(0)}}{4Eg_{R,21}^{(0)}+2\Delta g_{R,12}^{(0)}-2\Delta^{*}g_{R,12}^{(0)}}.

(99)

When
|\Delta|\ll|E|
,

g_{R,11}^{(1)}\approx i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot(\Delta^{*}\partial_{\bf R}\Delta-\Delta\partial_{\bf R}\Delta^{*})/(4E^{3})
.
g_{R,22}^{(1)}

has a similar structure. So,
g_{R,11}^{(1)}
and
g_{R,22}^{(1)}
are proportional to the square of
\Delta
, which can be ignored in the linear expansion in terms of
\partial_{\bf R}\Delta
.
Thereby, solving
\tilde{g}_{R}^{(1)}
only needs to retain the off-diagonal terms in the matrix.
Accordingly, from Eqs. (
98a
)-(
98c
), we find

\displaystyle g_{R,12}^{(1)}\approx-i/(2E^{2}){\bf v}_{F}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}\Delta,

(100a)

\displaystyle g_{R,21}^{(1)}\approx-i/(2E^{2}){\bf v}_{F}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}\Delta^{*}.

(100b)

The above analysis yields a straightforward iterative relation for the off-diagonal terms when
n\geq 2
, i.e.,

2Eg_{R,12}^{(n)}=-i{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\partial_{\bf R}g_{R,12}^{(n-1)}.

(101)

When
n=2
,

g_{R,12}\approx g_{R,12}^{(0)}+g_{R,12}^{(1)}+g_{R,12}^{(2)}=\frac{\Delta}{E}+\frac{|\Delta|^{2}\Delta}{2E^{3}}-\frac{i}{2E^{2}}{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial\Delta}{\partial{\bf R}}-\frac{1}{4E^{3}}{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\Big({\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial\Delta}{\partial{\bf R}}\Big).

(102)

On the other hand, we take the equilibrium distribution of
\tilde{h}
. Thus, according to Eqs. (
90
) and (
91
), we obtain

g_{K,12}=2\tanh\Big(\frac{\beta E}{2}\Big)\Big[\frac{\Delta}{E}+\frac{|\Delta|^{2}\Delta}{2E^{3}}-\frac{i}{2E^{2}}{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial\Delta}{\partial{\bf R}}-\frac{1}{4E^{3}}{\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf R}}\Big({\bf v}_{F}\cdot\frac{\partial\Delta}{\partial{\bf R}}\Big)\Big].

(103)

Substituting Eq. (
103
) into the gap equation (
92
) yields

\Delta=\frac{2\pi N_{0}|\lambda|}{\beta}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{\Delta}{\omega_{n}}-\frac{|\Delta|^{2}\Delta}{2\omega_{n}^{3}}+\frac{v_{F}^{2}}{12\omega_{n}^{3}}\frac{\partial^{2}\Delta}{\partial{\bf R}^{2}}\Big).

(104)

Here, two types of summation should be performed, noting that the truncation of the Debye frequency is needed in the summation over the Matsubara frequency [see Eq. (
97
)]. For the first summation

\frac{2\pi N_{0}|\lambda|}{\beta}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Delta}{\omega_{n}}=\frac{2\pi N_{0}|\lambda|}{\beta}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{M_{0}(T_{c})}\frac{\Delta}{\omega_{n}}+\frac{2\pi N_{0}|\lambda|}{\beta}\sum\limits_{M_{0}(T_{c})}^{M_{0}(T)}\frac{\Delta}{\omega_{n}}=\Delta+\Delta N_{0}|\lambda|\ln\Big(\frac{T_c}{T}\Big);

(105)

for the second and third summations,

\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega_{n}^{3}}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{3}T^{3}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n+1/2)^{2}}=\frac{7}{8\pi^{3}T^{3}}\zeta(3),

(106)

in which the formula
\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}{1}/{(n+1/2)^{z}}=(2^{z}-1)\zeta(z)
is used
[
265
]
. Here
\zeta(z)
is the Riemann function
[
265
]
.
Finally, Eq. (
104
) is written as

\frac{\pi}{8T_{c}}\frac{7\zeta(3)v_{F}^{2}}{6\pi^{3}T_{c}}\frac{\partial^{2}\Delta}{\partial{\bf R}^{2}}+\ln\Big(\frac{T_c}{T}\Big)\Delta-\frac{7\zeta(3)}{8\pi^{2}T^{2}}|\Delta|^{2}\Delta=0,

(107)

which is the Ginzburg-Landau equation for the conventional superconductivity
[
79
]
.

2.4.5
Electrodynamics of superconductors

The general electrodynamics of superconductors relies on the frequency of the electromagnetic fields
[
96
,
97
,
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
,
112
]
